Hi, doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me. I, um... I hope I'm in the right place, and I really don't want to waste your time. 

It's just... well, I've been having a little trouble at work lately. I'm a carpenter, and I just can't seem to keep my mind on one thing anymore. It's a bit embarrassing to admit, and it's probably just me being silly, but... like, if just one saw starts up in the shop, or somebody walks past the office, or even if I just have a passing thought about another job, it completely pulls me off what I’m doing. And then, before I know it, the whole day just sort of gets away from me. 

I try really hard to stay on track, I promise I do. Is that... does that make sense to you? I hope I'm explaining it right.

Oh, um... well, I really try to be respectful, especially in places where you're supposed to be quiet. I hope it's okay to admit this, but... I do struggle a little bit with that.

Oh, um... schoolwork. Well, I really always tried my best to do a good job. I hope that's okay to say. I didn't want the teachers to think I wasn't trying, because I really was. 

But, um... I guess maybe sometimes I didn't always catch every little thing? I just... sometimes my eyes would sort of gloss over the small stuff, even when I was trying so hard to be careful.

Oh, um... well, as an adult... I don't think it's always been quite this noticeable. I really hope that's the right thing to say. I've always been a hard worker, you know? Being a carpenter, you're always moving, working with your hands, and I've always been very good at that. 

But, um... I guess things have gotten a bit harder lately. Well, for about a year and a month now. I, um, I had a bit of an accident and broke my wrist, which was just so silly of me, I felt terrible about it. Because of that, they had to shift me off the hands-on work and put me on shop paperwork and supervising. 

And I've really, really tried my best to do a good job in the new role, I promise I have. But sitting at a desk with all those forms and schedules... that's when I really started noticing how much I struggle to keep my focus and sit still. It's just been since that change, really. I hope that makes sense? I'm sorry if I'm rambling, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the timeline you need. Does that help answer your question, doctor?

I really hate to complain, because I know everyone gets a little scattered sometimes, but... it's actually been kind of hard. I mean, I'm sure I'm overreacting a bit, but... I've made a few silly mistakes lately. Just little things, mostly! But, um, they did end up causing some delays, and... well, I had to redo a couple of jobs. I felt terrible about it, I really did. And I think... I think the crew is maybe a little less eager to rely on me for things like scheduling or ordering materials right now. Which is fair! I don't blame them at all. \n\nAnd, um, at home... my husband is wonderful, he really is, but he's had to take over paying the bills and keeping track of our reminders. I just kept misplacing the papers, or... or I'd completely forget what needed to be done. I feel awful putting that on him. I'm trying to do better, I promise. Do you think... is that the sort of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're trying to sit still?\n\nPatient: Well, um... maybe just a little? I hope it doesn't sound like I'm acting like a child who can't sit in her chair. I know I should be better at it at my age. \n\nBut... if I have to sit at the desk doing estimates, or, um, if I have to sit through a really long meeting, I do get this sort of... wound up feeling inside. It's like I just really need to get up and move around. Even though my wrist is healed up now, so I don't really have a good excuse to be taking breaks, and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish what I'm doing. \n\nI really do try to just sit quietly and be good, I promise. Does that... is that what you meant? I just don't want you to think I'm doing it on purpose to be disruptive or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't really think so. I mean, I hope I'm not misunderstanding what you mean, but... I always try to be really careful. \n\nI know I get a bit scattered, like I mentioned, but I don't usually just... jump into things or make snap decisions. Especially at the shop, you know, I really try to be safe and follow all the rules so I don't cause any accidents or make things harder for anyone else. If anything, I probably worry a little too much about making sure I do things the right way so I don't upset the rest of the crew. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I act on impulse. Is that... um, is that helpful? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the information you need, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, not exactly running around, I don't think. I hope it's okay if I say that. \n\nBut... even back in grade school, you know, before I was 12 or so... my teachers used to have to talk to my mother a lot. They were always very nice about it, but they'd tell her that I was bright, but... well, a little careless. I hated hearing that, because I really wanted to be a good student! But I was always losing my homework, or, um, forgetting the instructions they had just given us. And I'd end up just staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. \n\nI really didn't mean to ignore them or be disrespectful, I promise. I just... drifted off a lot. I hope I'm answering your question right, since I wasn't exactly bouncing out of my chair. I try so hard to just lock onto the piece I'm working on, but every little noise or movement from the other crew members just pulls me right out of it. I didn't want them to think I was a slob or anything. \n\nI know I mentioned losing my homework before, and I feel terrible about that, but I really did try to keep my things together. I just... things would slip my mind sometimes. But I wouldn't say I was completely disorganized, you know? I hope I'm making sense, doctor. I'm trying to remember as best I can so I can give you the right information. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... no, I really don't think so. I hope I'm not being difficult by saying no again. I just... I really try to be careful and think things through. 

Especially with my work—I'm a carpenter, so, well, you really can't afford to just act on a whim when you're dealing with power tools and expensive wood. You have to be very deliberate, you know? Measure twice, cut once. I always try to be responsible and do things the right way. I would absolutely hate for anyone to think I was reckless or unpredictable, so I'm always very cautious about my choices. 

So, no, I don't make impulsive decisions. I hope that's okay? Am I giving you enough information to work with, doctor? I really want to make sure I'm doing this right for you.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Oh, goodness, no. Um... I hope I'm not being unhelpful with all these no's. But I really wasn't like that at all.

But sitting at a desk with all those forms and schedules... that's when I really started noticing how much I struggle to keep my focus and sit still. It's just been since that change, really. I hope that makes sense? I'm sorry if I'm rambling, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the timeline you need. Does that help answer your question, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention and restlessness since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, if we're talking about when I was very young... I suppose there were some things, even back then. I really hope it doesn't sound like I was a bad child, because I wasn't, I promise. I always wanted to be good. \n\nBut, um... even back in grade school, you know, before I was 12... my teachers used to talk to my mother a lot. They'd always say I was a bright girl, which was very kind of them! But they also said I was a bit... careless, I guess? I'd lose my homework, or forget the instructions they just gave us. And instead of finishing my seatwork, I'd end up just staring out the window. \n\nI didn't mean to be disrespectful to the teachers, I really didn't. It's just that my mind would wander off, even when I was trying my absolute hardest to be a good student. Is that... is that the kind of childhood thing you were looking for, doctor? I hope I'm answering this right for you.\n\nDoctor: Did you often run, climb, or play vigorously as a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not really. I hope it's alright to say that. I was actually a very quiet little girl. I really didn't want to cause any trouble or be a bother to my parents, you know? \n\nSo I wasn't one of those children bouncing off the walls or climbing up the furniture and getting into things. I always tried to be very well-behaved and just stay where I was supposed to. I didn't have that kind of wild physical energy at all. I'd mostly just sit quietly. Even if my mind was off somewhere else, my body was always still, I promise. \n\nIs that... does that help answer the question, doctor? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel like you were fidgeting or had excess energy when you were a child, even if you didn't act on it physically?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so. I really don't remember feeling like that. I hope that's okay to say. Like I mentioned, I was a very quiet and still child. I didn't feel like I had all this pent-up energy inside me or anything like that. \n\nI always wanted to make sure I wasn't being disruptive, so I just... sat quietly. I didn't feel restless or fidgety on the inside, either. My mind might have been daydreaming, but I didn't feel that sort of nervous energy you're talking about. I really tried my best to just be calm and good. Does that make sense, doctor? I hope I'm not making things too difficult to figure out.\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel like you needed to be active or had trouble sitting still during quiet activities as a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... I never had trouble sitting still during quiet time. \n\nI was actually very happy to just sit there. I didn't feel any sort of... urge to get up and move around or anything like that. I always wanted to do exactly what the teacher asked, so if it was time to be quiet and sit, I stayed perfectly still in my chair. Oh... yes, I'm afraid so. I really wish I could tell you it was just a phase, but it's been happening for as long as I can remember. I hope it's alright that I'm sharing this, it's just a little embarrassing to admit that I've always struggled like this.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, no, not really. I hope I'm not making things sound worse than they are, but... no, it's never really gone away. 

I know some people grow out of things, and I always hoped I would, too. I tried so hard to just... be better about it, you know? To be more organized and focused, especially when I started working.
